# Daily Arxiv Tools
Keywords: Time Series, Spatio Temporal, Time Series Imputation, Irregular Time Series, Diffusion Model, Graph Neural Networks

The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-24

| **[Volatility-Corrected Prediction Intervals: Locally Adaptive Uncertainty Quantification for Probabilistic Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31621v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prediction intervals from probabilistic time-series forecasters are commonly assessed by marginal coverage, the long-run proportion of observations falling inside the intervals. However, forecast errors in time series are often serially dependent and heteroskedastic, so prediction intervals can attain near-nominal marginal coverage while miscovering systematically during periods of larger local error variance. We identify and address this failure mode, which we call \emph{local scale miscalibration}. We first document the phenomenon using time-series foundation models (TSFMs): across several real-world series, their native intervals often attain near-nominal marginal coverage while exhibiting substantial temporal variation in local coverage, alternating between overcoverage and undercoverage over shorter windows. We then propose Volatility-Corrected Prediction Intervals (VCPI), a post-hoc calibration procedure that uses the base forecaster's predictive scale as a baseline, models the volatility of standardized forecast residuals with a Generalized Autoregressive Conditional Heteroskedasticity (GARCH) model, and applies a multiplicative correction to produce locally adaptive prediction intervals. We justify VCPI both theoretically and empirically, comparing it against model-agnostic conformal counterparts. We provide a theoretical analysis of interval adaptation of the proposed method following abrupt changes in volatility. Empirically, we evaluate VCPI on Chronos, Moirai, and TimesFM across four real-world datasets and find that it produces more stable local coverage than conformal baselines while maintaining near-nominal marginal coverage and comparable interval width.</p></details> |  |
| **[Are EEG Sequences Time Series? EEG Classification with Time Series Models and Joint Subject Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.06966v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As with most other data domains, EEG data analysis relies on rich domain-specific preprocessing. Beyond such preprocessing, machine learners would hope to deal with such data as with any other time series data. For EEG classification many models have been developed with layer types and architectures we typically do not see in time series classification. Furthermore, typically separate models for each individual subject are learned, not one model for all of them. In this paper, we systematically study the differences between EEG classification models and generic time series classification models. We describe three different model setups to deal with EEG data from different subjects, subject-specific models (most EEG literature), subject-agnostic models and subject-conditional models. In experiments on three datasets, we demonstrate that off-the-shelf time series classification models trained per subject perform close to EEG classification models, but that do not quite reach the performance of domain-specific modeling. Additionally, we combine time-series models with subject embeddings to train one joint subject-conditional classifier on all subjects. The resulting models are competitive with dedicated EEG models in 2 out of 3 datasets, even outperforming all EEG methods on one of them.</p></details> |  |
| **[DISC: Domain-Integrated Similarity Cost for Time-Series Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31288v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The growth of time-series datasets and foundation models has made dataset comparison a central part of time-series workflows. Assessing source-target transfer before model adaptation requires similarity estimates that can summarize transfer-related dataset relationships without exhaustive source-target evaluation over every candidate pair. Many metrics have been proposed for measuring similarity between time-series datasets, but they capture different dataset relationships and vary in how well they align with downstream utility across tasks. We evaluate this gap by measuring the correlation between source-target similarity scores and downstream forecasting errors in out-of-distribution and transfer-learning settings. We propose Domain-Integrated Similarity Cost (DISC), a learnable aggregation framework that uses calibration transfer observations to select, weight, and combine similarity metrics for different downstream tasks. By integrating complementary metric values, DISC better distinguishes source-target dataset differences that matter for downstream behavior. After calibration, DISC scores source-target pairs using only their similarity-metric values and yields higher alignment than single-metric references on the reported split. These results show that time-series dataset similarity is task-dependent, and that learned metric integration provides a stronger proxy for downstream transfer behavior than manually selecting one similarity metric.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted as a conference paper at IEEE BigData 2026</p></details> |
| **[QuaNAS: Uncertainty-Aware Reuse of Earlier Searches for Query-Efficient Neural Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31251v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Neural architecture search (NAS) on a new dataset often affords only tens of short training runs, so what the search knows before its first evaluation decides its efficiency. Yet most NAS methods start from scratch, guided at best by zero-cost proxies that are unreliable across tasks. QuaNAS treats a library of earlier searches in the same search space as explicit knowledge of uncertain reliability. Main effects of design decisions learned from this library and, secondarily, zero-cost proxies become priors over candidate architectures without any label of the new dataset. A Gaussian process starts at the prior optimum and re-estimates after every evaluation how far each prior is trusted. Under a protocol frozen before any confirmation data were seen, QuaNAS was evaluated once on 12 new image, time-series, text and tabular datasets. It lowered anytime regret on the short-training objective 2.7- to 3.9-fold relative to five pre-specified NAS baselines that start from scratch (Holm-adjusted p = 0.0012 each). Its final models matched (non-inferiority margin 0.5 pp) or exceeded matched-recipe reference models on nine datasets, as did the prior optimum, selected without a single evaluation. Post-hoc comparisons with established transfer methods given the same library trace the gain to the library: without it, the same Gaussian process had over twice the regret. They also show where each transfer mechanism works best: the pooled main effects of QuaNAS for weakly transferable tabular tasks, per-task surrogates for image, time-series and text tasks and for small or mismatched libraries. Finally, the main effects make the transferred knowledge inspectable.</p></details> | <details><summary>30 pa...</summary><p>30 pages, 7 figures, 13 tables. Code and data: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23562488</p></details> |
| **[Towards Field-Deployable Wearable IMU Systems for Occupational Activity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31160v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Wearable inertial measurement unit (IMU) systems for construction worker human activity recognition (HAR) routinely report aggregate accuracies above 95 %, rarely under genuine subject-independent (leave-one-subject-out (LOSO)) evaluation, masking the per-individual variance that determines real-world deployment performance. Using the VTT-ConIoT benchmark, we conduct, to our knowledge, the first systematic configuration sensitivity analysis of this dataset: six classifiers across five sensor placements and three window sizes under a single LOSO protocol, a personalised-calibration pilot, and a validated comparison of tabular GAN-based and standard time-series augmentation. Per-subject LOSO accuracy for the main multi-sensor, 16-class configuration ranges from 61.9 % to 88.7 % (mean 77.5 %), a 27 pp spread, though all data are laboratory-collected. Multi-sensor fusion and longer windows both improve accuracy; standard augmentation gives small, consistent gains, while Conditional Tabular GAN (CTGAN) augmentation actively harms accuracy with a demonstrated distributional-fidelity mechanism; and personalised calibration recovers 16.8 pp for the weakest participants from just 30 % of their own data. These findings provide evidence-grounded configuration guidance for scaling occupational body sensor networks towards field deployment</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at IEEE BSN 2026</p></details> |
| **[Zero-Shot Forecasting of In-Situ Soil Moisture and Temperature with a Time-Series Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30971v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Soil moisture and temperature govern plant-water availability and microbial activity, making them key indicators for evaluating land-restoration efforts on degraded landscapes. However, the physical and data-driven models normally used to forecast them both need information that a newly instrumented restoration site does not yet have: hydraulic parameters for the former and months of per-site training data for the latter. We evaluate TimesFM 1, a 500M-parameter time-series foundation model, zero-shot on 15-minute telemetry from 27 sensor stations across three land-management regimes (Control, Treatment, Scald) at a semi-arid restoration site, forecasting 5 cm and 30 cm soil moisture and enclosure temperature at seven lead times from 1 h to 168 h. Using gap-aware rolling-origin evaluation across sensors, we find that TimesFM beats the best rule-based statistical forecasting baselines (persistence, seasonal-naive, or 7-day climatology) by 21-76% at sub-daily lead times but loses by 6-79% beyond a day, a pattern explained by a diurnal-alignment mechanism that holds for both soil-moisture depths and temperature. A CatBoost baseline trained walk-forward on the same origins with full history and weather covariates never wins overall, and we show that its weakness is partly a cold-start effect specific to short deployments. We additionally quantify the inference cost of zero-shot forecasting on commodity CPU hardware. Our results highlight a practical trade-off between zero-shot forecasting capability, predictive performance, and computational efficiency.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Tackling Climate Change with Machine Learning</p></details> |
| **[Energy and Latency First: How Deployment Constraints Reverse Time-Series Model Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30965v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI models, and time-series models in particular are typically ranked by accuracy and deployed under constraints absent from that ranking. In this paper, we study a real setting where these constraints are explicit and measurable: handover pre- diction in cellular networks, where inference runs on the User Equipment once per 40 ms measurement period and must complete before the network acts. The timing bound is hard: a late prediction is superseded by the measurement it was predicting, while energy is a cumulative budget spent for the life of the device. Neither appears in the objective these models are selected on. We evaluate eight architectures under a protocol that screens for deadline feasibility at the tail rather than the mean, then ranks the feasible models on measured inference energy, count- ing the feature computation that precedes the forward pass and is usually omitted. Gradient-boosted trees on handcrafted temporal features are the most accurate model (F1=0.974) and among the cheapest to run, while the neural architecture an accuracy-only benchmark would rank first (CNN-1D, F1=0.914) costs over 3 times more energy per inference under a realistic duty-cycled deployment pattern. We give three conditions under which this outcome should be expected, so the finding applies beyond telecommunications. Accuracy-only benchmarking does not merely under-report deployment cost. It can rank first a model that cannot be deployed.</p></details> | 13 pages, 1 figure |
| **[Random Initialization as a Baseline for Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30950v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time series foundation models (TSFMs) use self-supervised pretraining to learn reusable representations for downstream tasks such as multivariate time series classification (MTSC). We examine how much pretrained weights contribute beyond the architecture itself by comparing seven publicly released instances from four TSFM families with architecture-matched randomly initialized counterparts on the UEA MTSC archive. For each instance, five random initializations are evaluated using four classifiers on frozen representations: ridge classification, logistic regression, RBF SVM, and random forest. After model-specific exclusions due to computational constraints, the study comprises 183 paired dataset-level comparisons. Pretraining improves mean accuracy for all seven instances, but the gains are modest, ranging from 1.43 to 5.19 percentage points, with an overall average improvement of 2.58 points. Three of seven per-instance Wilcoxon signed-rank tests have raw $p$-values below 0.05, but only NuTime remains significant after Holm correction. A secondary pooled test across all 183 pairs gives $p < 0.001$, while the corrected per-instance tests remain the primary evidence. We also compare with ROCKET, Hydra, and Quant, which remain competitive without requiring large-scale pretraining. These findings suggest that MTSC evaluations should distinguish the effect of pretrained weights from that of the underlying architecture and should include both architecture-matched random initialization and strong non-pretrained baselines.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by the NeurIPS-26 Workshop on TS-LIMITS</p></details> |
| **[Future of Time Series Anomaly Detection: From Historical Review to Unified Outlook](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30628v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time series anomaly detection (TSAD) is a foundational problem in data mining, aiming to identify observations that deviate significantly from nominal temporal patterns. Driven by escalating data volumes and application complexity, recent research has yielded diverse architectures, including diffusion models, state space models, and large language models (LLMs). However, existing surveys provide limited coverage of these emerging designs and lack a unified treatment of multivariate anomalies, cross-domain unified modeling, metric reliability, and contemporary evaluation challenges. To bridge these gaps, this survey delivers three core contributions: (1) We establish a refined anomaly categorization and a structured taxonomy of paradigms reflecting modern advances, comparing detection mechanisms and charting developmental trajectories across paradigms. (2) We provide an extensive analysis of evaluation methodologies and conduct comprehensive experiments to assess metric fidelity, complemented by an open-source benchmark repository encompassing algorithms, datasets, and evaluation tools. (3) We outline key open challenges, offering forward-looking perspectives on dynamic thresholding, model interpretability, zero-shot transfer, multi-modal alignment, and foundational models for time series. By addressing existing theoretical and empirical limitations, this survey establishes a coherent framework to guide future TSAD research and practical deployments.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Accepted by IEEE Transactions on Artificial Intelligence. DOI: 10.1109/TAI.2026.3746935</p></details> |
| **[TT-SAX: A Two-Track Time Series Symbolization for Natural Language Shape Querying](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30593v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Natural language querying for time series databases aims to let users find what they need in long histories of data by asking in plain language. Shape queries ask how a series moves. They are the main challenge of this task. Recent work makes them feasible by using symbolization as a bridge between the query and the long history. However, existing symbolizations were designed for compression, similarity indexing, or classification. What a symbolization should provide for natural language querying has not been systematically studied. We therefore examine the definitions of six classical symbolizations against this task and identify three limitations. (1) Direction is missing from the string, (2) direction is measured at too small a scale, and (3) the letters are arbitrary. Three design principles follow. We follow these principles and make a minimal redesign of SAX called TT-SAX (two-track SAX). The level letters stay. Each segment gains a trend letter. It is measured across neighboring segments rather than inside one, and graded to match the adjectives that queries use. We conduct extensive experiments on the public NLQTSBench benchmark, comparing TT-SAX with the six classical symbolizations across six LLMs. TT-SAX outperforms all six on shape queries, and ablations trace its gains back to the three limitations one by one. This paper is the first systematic study of time series symbolization with natural language querying as the design goal. It provides a foundation for future research on this task. Our code is available at https://github.com/Atlamtiz/tt-sax.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 8 figures, 2 tables. Accepted at the Australasian Database Conference (ADC 2026). This is the submitted manuscript prior to peer review</p></details> |
| **[WhatIf-Bench: Benchmarking Intervention-Aware Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26582v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent time series foundation models (TSFMs) can consume covariates in-context, extending zero-shot forecasting to problems driven by external factors. This enables scenario analysis: forecasting how a target responds to events that set covariates to chosen future values. Whether they can do this reliably is an open question. We introduce WhatIf-Bench, a benchmark measuring how well forecasters use covariates to forecast a target's response to future events. WhatIf-Bench is semi-synthetic: time series come from mechanistic simulators of data centers, power grids, road traffic, and water networks, with controlled covariate-target relationships grounded in each system. Each task presents a history of events intervening on covariates, then asks to forecast a future event that either replays seen values (Replay) or sets them to unseen ones (Novel). Varying the number of covariates, historical events, and event length yields 37,665 tasks. Evaluating TSFMs, frontier LLMs, and simple regression baselines, we find the LLMs reach the highest skill (improvement over a naive baseline), ahead of the best TSFMs. Every model forecasts well on Replay futures (48 to 85% skill) but drops to -7 to 50% on Novel futures, with the regression baselines falling the most. Skill on Novel futures also falls as events set more covariates jointly. More historical events raise skill on Novel futures across the board, but large differences remain. WhatIf-Bench reveals generalization to Novel futures as the core challenge, and offers a testbed toward arbitrary scenario analysis.</p></details> | <details><summary>Leade...</summary><p>Leaderboard at https://huggingface.co/spaces/arjunashok/WhatIf-Bench and code at https://github.com/ashok-arjun/whatif-bench</p></details> |
| **[SCDM: Scalable Causal Discovery in Nonlinear Temporal Systems with Meta-Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29992v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Causal discovery from nonlinear multivariate time series is challenging in high-dimensional systems, where the number of candidate directed relations grows quadratically with the number of variables. Existing methods are often limited by target-wise model fitting, repeated conditional testing, or expensive graph-search procedures. We propose SCDM, a shared-parameter meta-learning framework for scalable temporal causal discovery. SCDM treats each target variable as a task while learning a shared temporal predictor and a shared causal-strength matrix across tasks. Each meta-episode updates only a subset of target-variable tasks, allowing the model to accumulate structural evidence across the full system without exhaustive target-wise optimization. After training, a post-training graph readout converts the learned predictor into continuous directed causal scores for threshold-independent evaluation. We also provide a finite-error recovery principle showing that, under fully observed delayed-system assumptions and a positive risk-gap condition, thresholding SCDM scores recovers the graph when statistical, meta-optimization, and readout errors are below the population margin. Experiments on controlled synthetic, neuroimaging-inspired, high-dimensional, and real-world temporal benchmarks demonstrate competitive causal recovery and improved scalability in large temporal systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. 44 pages, 6 figures</p></details> |
| **[Synthesis and Verification of Register Automata over Data Sequences (Technical Report)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.19100v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Data sequences, such as time series, traces, and sensor streams, are words over large or infinite data domains. Their properties often depend on comparisons between values at different positions, which finite automata cannot express directly. We study synthesis and verification for Deterministic Register Automata (DRAs) over ordered data sequences. DRAs combine finite control with registers for remembered data values, providing compact symbolic descriptions of quantitative sequence languages. Our technical core is a local-search synthesis procedure and an exact robustness checker for DRAs. We fix the alphabet theory to ordered rationals $(\Q;<,=)$ with rational guard constants and use non-negative affine accumulator costs. For a fixed number of DRA registers, the checker runs in polynomial time; its dependence on that number is exponential. It reduces robustness to coverability and then to a shortest-path problem. Since the search ranges over $\Q^+$, it produces variable-length witnesses and, for RAA-definable alignment costs such as DTW, explores a counterexample space with no a priori length bound. As an application, we use learned DRAs as interpretable surrogates for queried neural sequence classifiers. A probably approximately correct (PAC) validation loop refines rejected candidates, returns genuine non-robustness witnesses for the classifier when found, and accepts a surrogate only with statistical guarantees for fidelity and local stability. We implement the framework in $\textsc{RAExc}$, enabling the synthesis of interpretable automata and the verification of their robustness. We evaluate the approach on benchmark languages and sequentialized MNIST, demonstrating high accuracy while verifying the robustness of the learned automata.</p></details> |  |
| **[Leveraging Time-Series Foundation Models for Financial Crime Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29667v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Financial crime detection requires identifying illicit activity within large transaction networks, where suspicious behaviour is rare and unfolds over time. Existing graph-based methods capture relationships among accounts but typically underuse the temporal behaviour encoded in account transaction histories. We introduce Temporal-Context Edge Enrichment (TCEE), a framework that addresses this gap by summarising each account's transaction history with a frozen time-series foundation model (TSFM), and enriching each transaction's representation with the relevant temporal context from these summaries. These enriched representations can be scored directly for edge classification, or fed into a graph neural network (GNN) as edge features to capture broader structural information. Across six AMLWorld datasets, TCEE improves PR-AUC over the strongest evaluated baseline on five by a relative 10.8% on average, while training 6.0x faster end-to-end. Feeding TCEE's representations into a GNN raises PR-AUC a further 4.8% on the four smaller datasets. On an Ethereum phishing dataset, we show that TSFM-generated account representations fed into a GNN are also effective for node classification, improving PR-AUC by 14.5% over the strongest baseline. As TSFMs continue to advance, our results suggest they can strengthen financial crime detection by capturing temporal signals that complement graph-based approaches.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 5 figures, 5 tables. Accepted at the ACM International Conference on AI in Finance (ICAIF '26), Milan, Italy</p></details> |
| **[Rethinking Agentic Model Orchestration for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29504v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time series foundation models (TSFMs) perform unevenly across forecasting tasks. Recent work uses agentic model orchestration (AMO) to select or combine TSFMs, often outperforming individual forecasters. Yet these gains may reflect stronger or more complementary candidates rather than better orchestration. To disentangle these effects, we introduce FairAMO, a controlled evaluation protocol with frozen candidate forecasts. Experiments on GIFT-Eval show that AMO improves accuracy, but its gains are bounded by the candidate TSFMs. The best orchestrators reduce MASE by less than 6% relative to the best single TSFM across all tasks. These gains depend on complementarity (different candidates excel on different tasks) rather than the number of candidates. Adding members yields diminishing returns, and a redundant member can erase these gains. The value of historical evidence also saturates quickly. A few recent windows capture all gains, and further history adds none. These findings separate system-level forecasting gains from the contribution of orchestration itself.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Foundation Models for Temporal Systems (FMTS)</p></details> |
| **[Accurate and Efficient Multi-Channel Time Series Forecasting via Sparse Attention Mechanism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18712v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The task of multi-channel time series forecasting is ubiquitous in numerous fields such as finance, supply chain management, and energy planning. It is critical to effectively capture complex dynamic dependencies within and between channels for accurate predictions. However, traditional method paid few attentions on learning the interaction among channels. This paper proposes Linear-Network (Li-Net), a novel architecture designed for multi-channel time series forecasting that captures the linear and non-linear dependencies among channels. Li-Net dynamically compresses representations across sequence and channel dimensions, processes the information through a configurable non-linear module and subsequently reconstructs the forecasts. Moreover, Li-Net integrates a sparse Top-K Softmax attention mechanism within a multi-scale projection framework to address these challenges. A core innovation is its ability to seamlessly incorporate and fuse multi-modal embeddings, guiding the sparse attention process to focus on the most informative time steps and feature channels. Through the experiment results on multiple real-world benchmark datasets demonstrate that Li-Net achieves competitive performance compared to state-of-the-art baseline methods. Furthermore, Li-Net provides a superior balance between prediction accuracy and computational burden, exhibiting significantly lower memory usage and faster inference times. Detailed ablation studies and parameter sensitivity analyses validate the effectiveness of each key component in our proposed architecture. Keywords: Multivariate Time Series Forecasting, Sparse Attention Mechanism, Multimodal Information Fusion, Non-linear relationship</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ICDE 2026</p></details> |
| **[A spatiotemporal Bayesian hierarchical model of heat-related mortality in Catalonia, Spain (2012--2022): The role of environmental and socioeconomic modifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.17148v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extreme heat represents a growing threat to public health as climate change intensifies. While its mortality impacts are well recognised, the interplay between heat, air pollution, and socioeconomic vulnerability remains insufficiently understood. We quantified the associations of extreme maximum temperatures and heatwaves with daily mortality in Catalonia (2012-2022) and evaluated how air pollutants-particularly ozone, nitrogen dioxide and particulate matter-and socioeconomic factors confound or modify this relationship. We conducted a time-series ecological analysis across 379 Basic Health Areas during summer months, linking mortality with meteorological, air pollution and socioeconomic indicators. Hierarchical Bayesian zero-inflated negative binomial models were fitted with nested spatiotemporal random effects (Matérn SPDE field evolving over years; month nested within year; unstructured ABS frailty). Among 210.860 summer deaths in the complete environmental sample, extreme heat and heatwaves were associated with higher mortality after multi-pollutant and socioeconomic adjustment (e.g., extreme heat at lag 2: RR $1.105$, 95% CrI $1.086$-$1.125$; heatwave at lag 3: RR $1.142$, 95% CrI $1.116$-$1.167$; posterior Prob $\approx 1$). Ozone concentrations $\geq 100\,μ$g/m$^3$ were positively associated with mortality, and extreme heat$\times$high ozone interactions further elevated risk. Areas with higher income inequality and older population structures also showed elevated mortality. These findings indicate that heat indicators raise summer mortality in Catalonia even after environmental and socioeconomic adjustment, while compound ozone exposure and contextual vulnerability further shape risk. Adaptation strategies should therefore combine heat-health warnings with air quality management and equity-oriented interventions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Time-Uniform Dependent Central Limit Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29288v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We establish a near-optimal almost sure invariance principle for nonstationary time series under the functional dependence measure (FDM) framework. This result yields a universal time-uniform dependent central limit theorem, which serves as the basis for anytime-valid inference. In particular, we instantiate the theorem through asymptotic confidence sequences (AsympCSs), time-uniform counterparts of asymptotic confidence intervals obtained via central limit theorems, extending those originally proposed in Waudby-Smith et al. (2024) to time series with serial dependence. We further refine their definition because, as we show, it can admit AsympCSs that fail to achieve the desired asymptotic time-uniform coverage for a broad class of data-generating processes, including independent and identically distributed cases. We address this issue by adding a simple eventual-inclusion condition, which in turn permits more flexible variance estimation, particularly useful in time-series settings. For feasible implementation of AsympCSs, we establish strong consistency of HAC variance estimators under the FDM framework, accommodating a wider range of commonly used bandwidth choices than existing results under mixing conditions. As illustrations, we construct AsympCSs for algorithms with Markovian structure and time-series-based causal estimands.</p></details> |  |
| **[HealthMamba: An Uncertainty-aware Spatiotemporal Graph State Space Model for Effective and Reliable Healthcare Facility Visit Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05286v4)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Healthcare facility visit prediction is essential for optimizing healthcare resource allocation and informing public health policy. Despite advanced machine learning methods being employed for better prediction performance, existing works usually formulate this task as a time-series forecasting problem without considering the intrinsic spatial dependencies of different types of healthcare facilities, and they also fail to provide reliable predictions under abnormal situations such as public emergencies. To advance existing research, we propose HealthMamba, an uncertainty-aware spatiotemporal framework for accurate and reliable healthcare facility visit prediction. HealthMamba comprises three key components: (i) a Unified Spatiotemporal Context Encoder that fuses heterogeneous static and dynamic information, (ii) a novel Graph State Space Model called GraphMamba for hierarchical spatiotemporal modeling, and (iii) a comprehensive uncertainty quantification module integrating three uncertainty quantification mechanisms for reliable prediction. We evaluate HealthMamba on four large-scale real-world datasets from California, New York, Texas, and Florida. Results show HealthMamba achieves around 6.0% improvement in prediction accuracy and 3.5% improvement in uncertainty quantification over state-of-the-art baselines.</p></details> | IJCAI 2026 |
| **[Structured Temporal Causality for Interpretable Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.16511v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Real-world multivariate time series anomalies are rare and often unlabeled. Additionally, prevailing methods rely on increasingly complex architectures tuned to benchmarks, detecting only fragments of anomalous segments and overstating performance. In this paper, we introduce OracleAD, a simple and interpretable unsupervised framework for multivariate time series anomaly detection. OracleAD encodes each variable's past sequence into a single causal embedding to jointly predict the present time point and reconstruct the input window, effectively modeling temporal dynamics. These embeddings then undergo a self-attention mechanism to project them into a shared latent space and capture spatial relationships. These relationships are not static, since they are modeled by a property that emerges from each variable's temporal dynamics. The projected embeddings are aligned to a Stable Latent Structure (SLS) representing normal-state relationships. Anomalies are identified using a dual scoring mechanism based on prediction error and deviation from the SLS, enabling fine-grained anomaly diagnosis at each time point and across individual variables. Since any noticeable SLS deviation originates from embeddings that violate the learned temporal causality of normal data, OracleAD directly pinpoints the root-cause variables at the embedding level. OracleAD achieves state-of-the-art results across multiple real-world datasets and evaluation protocols, while remaining interpretable through SLS.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2025</p></details> |
| **[The dynamic generalized covariance measure for conditional independence testing with nonstationary time series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21647v5)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Identifying relationships among stochastic processes is a core objective in many fields, such as economics. While the standard toolkit for multivariate time series analysis has many advantages, it can be difficult to capture nonlinear dynamics using linear vector autoregressive models. This difficulty has motivated the development of methods for causal discovery and variable selection for nonlinear time series, which routinely employ tests for conditional independence. We audit a three-discriminator GAN with evolutionary weight adaptation (StatD2GAN) against re-implemented RCGAN and TimeGAN baselines on ERA5 weather sequences under a pre-specified protocol: the final two calendar years are held out behind a 168 hour embargo, calibration is fitted on the training block only, and the decision rules were frozen in advance. Evidence comes from 25 matched (location, seed) pairs across five locations, tested with Holm-corrected Wilcoxon signed-rank tests. First, isotonic calibration drives KS to a median of 1.019 times a per-location noise-and-shift floor, and neither baseline is distinguishable from StatD2GAN on it. Second, the sorted-representation discriminator is the only component whose removal significantly degrades cross-variable dependence (Kendall tau MAE +0.116, Holm p = 0.002); a rank-transformed variant also degrades it, consistent with quantile supervision rather than copula matching. Third, calibration raises physical constraint violations; projection removes them, but the largest marginal change (0.0110, Lhasa) exceeds the pre-specified tolerance in three locations. Fourth, the re-implemented TimeGAN is significantly better than StatD2GAN on Kendall tau and ACF MAE, RCGAN is worse on Kendall tau MAE and constraint violations, and the pre-specified collapse criterion was not met. We recommend floor-referenced marginal evaluation, matched-pair testing, and dependence and sequence-level metrics.</p></details> | <details><summary>Versi...</summary><p>Version 2: supersedes v1, whose results are retracted. Code and weights: doi:10.5281/zenodo.22996771 and doi:10.5281/zenodo.23480462. Derived from the first author's Master's thesis. 37 pages</p></details> |
| **[Meta-iLaD: Identifiable Latent Dynamics via Meta-Learning of Dynamics Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27180v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Learning latent dynamics is central to assessing the current states and forecasting the future trajectories of high-dimensional time series. For locally-stationary latent dynamics $\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{z}_{<t}; \mathbf{c})$ with latent dynamics state $\mathbf{z}_t$ and environment variable $\mathbf{c}$, prior identifiability results have largely focused on $\mathbf{z}_t$ when conditioned on predefined label $u$ of dynamics environments. This leaves two limitations: reliance on predefined labels that hinder generalization to unseen environments, and a limited understanding of the identifiability of $\mathcal{F}$ and $\mathbf{c}$ which -- although offering important structural properties for the identifiability of $\mathbf{z}_t$ -- are learned jointly with $\mathbf{z}_t$. We address these limitations with Meta-iLaD, a novel identifiable latent dynamics framework attained by meta-learning across dynamics environments. Meta-iLaD introduces a novel conditional prior of $\mathbf{c}$, modeled as a feedforward meta-learner to rapidly extract $\mathbf{c}$ from few-shot examples. Meta-iLaD further establishes identifiability for $\mathbf{z}_t$, $\mathbf{c}$, and $\mathcal{F}$ simultaneously, for a general formulation of $\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{z}_{< t};\mathbf{c})$ without restricting the dimension of $\mathbf{c}$ or how it modulates $\mathcal{F}$. On synthetic and real data, we provide strong empirical evidence that 1) conditioning on few-shot examples enables generalization to out-of-distribution environments, and 2) identifiability for $\mathbf{c}$ and $\mathcal{F}$ is critical for accurate forecasting beyond reconstructing observed trajectories. Code is available at https://github.com/GoForIt-YYB/Meta-iLaD.</p></details> | <details><summary>ICML ...</summary><p>ICML 2026. 23 pages, 9 figures</p></details> |
| **[Channel-Dependent State Space Model for Multivariate Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36453v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multivariate time series forecasting (MTSF) is critical across many real-world domains. By connecting selective SSMs with the Kalman filter, we leverage the missing measurement update in the former for cross-variable modeling while preserving the SSM backbone for robust temporal modeling. On strongly dependent ODE and PEMS datasets, Chameleon achieves the best MSE and MAE across all settings, while its CI ablation and prior CD methods incur 61-178% higher MSE on average. | **[Granular Instrumental Variables in Large Panels: Identification and Inference Across Strong, Nearly Weak, and Weak GIV](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02095v4)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Granular Instrumental Variables (GIV), introduced by Gabaix and Koijen (2024), use idiosyncratic shocks to large units as instruments for aggregate endogenous variables. First, I show that estimating the individual shocks used to construct the granular instrument has a first-order effect on the asymptotic variance of the GIV estimator. Existing methods do not account for this effect, leading to incorrect standard errors. I derive corrected inference. Second, I characterize how instrument strength changes as \(N\) grows, yielding strong, nearly-weak, and weak regimes. In the newly characterized nearly-weak regime, the instrument vanishes asymptotically but the estimator remains consistent and asymptotically normal, at a slower rate, when \(T\) grows sufficiently fast relative to \(N\). I apply the theory to estimate short-run supply and demand elasticities for crude oil, copper, and natural gas.</p></details> | <details><summary>Job m...</summary><p>Job market paper. 165 pages, 2 figures. JEL: C33, C36, C55, C38, C12, Q41</p></details> |

We investigate distributional properties of the process and discuss the maximum likelihood estimation (MLE). Additionally we analyze the copula moving aggregate process of order one, or MAG(1), as it is a basic building block. | **[REATS: LLM Reasoning-based Ensemble Learning for Adaptive Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10149v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Due to the diversity of real-world time series, no single forecasting model consistently dominates across all samples. We propose REATS, which leverages LLM reasoning capabilities as an intelligent ensemble router that jointly processes textual temporal pattern descriptions and numerical features to produce interpretable, sample-adaptive ensemble weights through chain-of-thought reasoning. To enable effective LLM-based ensembling, we study its key design choices and propose: (i) a structured input pipeline that transforms raw time series into hybrid textual--numerical representations with fixed token cost, enabling rule-based chain-of-thought construction without API dependency, augmented with retrieved similar-sample priors; (ii) a diverse multi-row weight supervision scheme coupled with a token-efficient percentage-table format that reduces numerical complexity and mitigates LLM hallucinations; and (iii) a two-stage fine-tuning framework combining SFT with GRPO, where a reciprocal reward mapping transforms the continuous unbounded MSE gap into bounded signals with amplified near-oracle sensitivity, addressing the uniform sensitivity and outlier-dominated advantage compression inherent in naive reward designs for regression-based GRPO. | **[Beyond Static Leaderboards: Capability-Focused Evaluation with Structural Diversity for Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26251v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Static public benchmarks for time series foundation models (TSFMs) typically have fixed and narrow test distributions. We propose CaFE (Capability-Focused Evaluation), a refreshable evaluation extension grounded in original benchmark data that enriches static benchmarks with structural diversity and enriches single-score black-box evaluation with multi-dimensional, capability-oriented assessment. CaFE first profiles the original benchmark and defines eight Structural Features (SFs) that characterize its temporal structure. For each SF, it resamples the original data according to its structural support, removes edge-of-distribution samples, and augments the retained samples by varying the SF's salience, producing samples with different structural profiles. After filtering out out-of-distribution samples while preserving diversity, CaFE yields a refreshed evaluation set. TSFMs are then evaluated on this set using both conventional forecast-error metrics and intervention-response metrics, giving each model a per-feature capability profile alongside endpoint accuracy. Experiments on GIFT-Eval and FEV-Mini20 show that CaFE yields stable evaluation results and reveals per-feature strengths and weaknesses that a single aggregate score hides. To establish the methodological foundation, we review the WGAN-LP formulation another variant model in this class and extend its framework through an adaptive kernel critic model. We then evaluate this extended model using power ratios, correlation coefficient (CC), and relative root mean squared error (RRMSE). The experimental results demonstrate that the proposed model outperforms benchmark methods in both accuracy and robustness. The evaluation shows promising performance of the WGAN-CLP with an adaptive kernel framework in forecasting sequence alterations across the voltage and energy time-series datasets.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Retrieval to Recognition:Knowledge-Enhanced Post-training of Time Series Foundation Models for Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26114v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time series foundation models (TSFMs) have achieved notable progress in classification tasks through large-scale pretraining. However, their post-training practice mainly focuses on implicit parameter fine-tuning, where task-relevant information is entangled with abundant irrelevant knowledge in vast parameter spaces. Rather than pretraining a new foundation model, R2R improves how existing TSFMs are post-trained: it introduces a decoupled query encoder to extract discriminative patterns and retrieve instance-level numerical knowledge from a constructed knowledge base. In this way, R2R allows TSFMs to recall task-relevant patterns learned during pretraining, improving the efficiency of knowledge utilization in downstream classification transfer. More precisely, we propose to model equilibrium conditions using causal mechanism time graphs. These (acyclic) graphs allow us to maintain the assumption of a simultaneous determination of prices and quantities and come with an interpretable notion of intervention. They further allow us to exploit recent advances in graphical inference to derive valid IV estimators that are consistent under weak assumptions. Finally, we argue that observing significant differences between the estimates of presumably valid estimators can help to reject false model assumptions, thereby improving our understanding of underlying economic dynamics. However, despite the considerable progress achieved by existing deep time series generators, two key challenges remain: (1) learning hierarchical representations for heterogeneous temporal patterns; and (2) coordinating the generation process of heterogeneous temporal dynamics. To address these key bottlenecks, in this work, we propose ORBIT, a unified deep generative framework for time series. Specifically, we develop two key components: the Frequency-Hierarchical VQ-VAE (FH-VQ-VAE) and the Frequency-Band Autoregressive Transformer (FBAR). The latter establishes a novel low-to-high frequency autoregressive direction, yielding a time series generation process that first constructs global temporal structures and then incrementally incorporates fine-grained temporal details. | **[Accelerated Drift of Scientific Prose in the LLM Era: Evidence from an Interrupted Time-Series Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26008v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Every scientific text is embedded in the writing norms of its time: it reflects earlier publications while also shaping the texts that follow. Using interrupted time-series regression, we find substantial post-2022 changes across multiple feature families and research domains, including increases in average word length, reduced formula-based readability, and declines in maximum dependency-tree depth and sentence-level depth variation. Individual features follow different dynamics: some lexical markers accelerate persistently, while others rise and then recede. Our findings show that isolated marker frequencies capture only part of the shift: changes in the rate, direction, and structure of linguistic trajectories provide complementary evidence of broader post-2022 change in scientific-preprint writing. Specifically, we first formalize System 1 sufficiency in time series reasoning, which motivates a compressed representation for time series with key frequency patterns preserved. To construct this representation, we derive a dimension reduction objective that combines (i) a sufficiency divergence that measures the core information lost during compression, and (ii) a realizability divergence that quantifies the incompatibility of the System 1 model to the optimal prediction given this representation. We optimize this objective by learning time series compression with a gating mechanism for sufficiency, and then aligning the selected representation with the embedding space for realizability. | **[STAR: Adaptive Spatial-Temporal Normalization for Unified Microservice Incident Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31645v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated incident management in large-scale microservice systems relies on learning robust representations from multimodal observability data, including metrics, logs, and traces. Unlike prior methods that treat normalization as static or task-agnostic, STAR formulates it as a learnable, context-conditioned transformation aligned with the intrinsic properties of microservice systems. The resulting adaptive representations are integrated into a unified self-supervised framework, enabling end-to-end unsupervised support for AD, FT, and RCL tasks. Extensive experiments on two real-world microservice benchmarks demonstrate that STAR consistently outperforms all state-of-the-art baselines, yielding significant and stable improvements across all three tasks. Our results highlight adaptive normalization as a principled and effective mechanism for robust multimodal representation learning in complex software systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Dynamic Dual-Feedback Conformal Inference for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26987v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Quantifying uncertainty in time series forecasting is particularly demanding because sequential data exhibit temporal dependence and are prone to distributional changes. Conformal inference has emerged as a powerful uncertainty quantification approach through the construction of prediction sets. Recent advances have introduced online conformal methods that adaptively adjust prediction thresholds through feedback mechanisms. However, the existing feedback mechanism typically relies solely on miscoverage indicators (actual feedback), whether the true label falls within the interval at each time step, while overlooking the empirical prediction threshold (estimated feedback) that is derived from the oracle conformal method. In this paper, we propose Dynamic Dual-feedback Conformal Inference (DDCI), which incorporates a dual-feedback mechanism consisting of actual feedback and estimated feedback. The former drives the primary adjustment of the intervals based on true observations, while the latter dampens excessive expansions or contractions by leveraging empirical thresholds from conformal inference during updates. By balancing these two signals, DDCI achieves more stable and narrower prediction intervals in sequential settings while maintaining near-nominal coverage in practice.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[ActTime: Action-Conditioned World Modeling for Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25764v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time-series models increasingly condition on known future inputs. In controlled systems, some of these inputs are actions that can be changed, compared, and optimized before execution. A model must therefore do more than predict the realized future: it must predict how outcomes change under different actions from the same observed history and support reliable comparison of candidate plans. We introduce ActTime, an action-conditioned world model for time series. Over the observed history, ActTime updates variable-specific latent states through an action-conditioned transition and corrects them using observations. It then rolls the same transition forward under candidate actions to generate future trajectories. Training uses only factual trajectories, without paired alternative-action outcomes or planning supervision. We construct a simulator-grounded evaluation suite across building energy, traffic, greenhouse climate, and inventory management, covering factual prediction, intervention-effect prediction, offline decision making, and closed-loop planning. ActTime ranks first in 12 of 16 domain-task settings. These results show that factual accuracy, intervention fidelity, and decision quality are related but not interchangeable.</p></details> |  |

However, due to the discrete nature of this set, such models struggle to capture smooth, variable-speed transitions, as well as stochastic mixtures of overlapping states (e.g., non-instantaneous state transitions), and the inferred dynamics often display spurious rapid switching on real-world datasets. Here, we propose the Gumbel Dynamical Model (GDM). First, by introducing a continuous relaxation of discrete states and a different noise model defined on the relaxed-discrete state space via the Gumbel distribution, GDM expands the set of available state dynamics, allowing the model to approximate smoother and non-stationary ground-truth dynamics more faithfully. Breaking from established literature, this new class uses a stochastic relaxed state to directly combine reusable dynamical primitives and govern observations, without a separate stochastic continuous trajectory. Second, the relaxation makes the model fully differentiable, enabling fast and scalable training with standard gradient descent methods. We validate our approach on standard simulation datasets and highlight its ability to model soft, sticky states and transitions in a stochastic setting. Furthermore, we apply our model to two real-world datasets, demonstrating its ability to infer interpretable states in stochastic time series with multiple dynamics, a setting where traditional methods often fail.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[Mask-Aware Execution for Efficient JEPA Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22674v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Joint Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) are becoming a core representation-learning primitive and a building block for latent world models across vision, video, audio, brain dynamics, and time series. Despite (potential of) wide deployment, current JEPA training pipelines are inefficient: each input is executed through multiple mask-specific branches, with redundant target-side work, and memory-bound token routing. These costs grow with the number of masks and limit GPU efficiency. We present M-JEPA, a mask-aware execution architecture that restructures JEPA training without changing the learning objective. M-JEPA separates mask-independent computation from mask-dependent routing, enabling shared context encoder execution, fused token routing and slicing with backward support, sparse target encoder execution over the union of target tokens, and masked patch embedding for sparse inputs. The resulting pipeline preserves training semantics while reducing computation, memory traffic, and synchronization overhead. We implement M-JEPA for five JEPA variants and evaluate it on NVIDIA A100 GPUs. Compared against the state-of-the-art baselines, M-JEPA achieves up to 1.7x end-to-end training speedup for 2-10 masks. Separately, with masked patch embedding, 4.75x patch-embedding speedup at high sparsity. These results show that execution restructuring, rather than changes to the JEPA objective, is a key lever for efficient JEPA training.</p></details> |  |
| **[CVTA: Cross-Variable Temporal Attention for Multivariate Irregular Time Series Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25584v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Irregularly sampled multivariate time series arise in many real-world domains such as healthcare, climate science, and large-scale monitoring systems. CVTA uses Variable-Conditioned Embeddings to project heterogeneous variables into variable-specific latent spaces, and applies temporal attention to capture cross-variable dependencies from this state. Furthermore, we demonstrate that CVTA can be integrated as an auxiliary module into existing state-of-the-art prediction models through a dynamic meta-decision model. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world benchmarks show that CVTA achieves strong short-term predictive accuracy, supports efficient online inference, improves existing prediction models when used as an auxiliary module, and learns attention patterns that align well with domain knowledge.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the Fortieth Annual Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2026), Main Track</p></details> |
| **[Online detection of distributional changes for time series in metric spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26422v3)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We propose an online testing framework for detecting distributional changes in serially dependent data with values in a separable metric space. Based on two-sample $U$-statistics, the framework encompasses sequential analogs of energy distance and maximum mean discrepancy (MMD) procedures while accommodating temporal dependence. We further establish new spectral approximation results for kernel matrices formed from serially dependent observations, and use them to construct a feasible Monte Carlo calibration procedure. Our flexible window construction encompasses classical, Page-type, and full-scan historical-baseline monitoring and can achieve short detection delays for both early and late changepoints, without requiring sub-Gaussianity or high-order moments of the raw observations. Simulations show reliable false-alarm control across linear, nonlinear, high-dimensional, and functional time-series models and further demonstrate that, over a broad range of alternatives and changepoint locations, the proposed method can achieve substantially shorter delays than recent procedures designed specifically for rapid detection. | **[FracTS: Hierarchical and Autoregressive Time Series Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25523v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time series are ubiquitous across different domains, and the growing use of synthetic data for privacy-preserving sharing, data augmentation, and stress testing makes time series generation increasingly important. Inspired by fractal generative modeling developed for images, FracTS adapts the paradigm from 2D to 1D sequences by learning a multi-scale representation that captures global trajectory structure at coarse scales and local dynamics at fine scales while following the autoregressive nature of time series. In contrast to unstructured images, time series are structured, allowing easy automated or designated temporal feature processing optimized for the model, and global (static) feature extraction describing the instances that generate the corresponding time series in the collection. We incorporate them in the generation process to further improve the generation quality. The design naturally handles multivariate dependencies and improves long-horizon temporal coherence without requiring fixed-length inputs. Experiments on five datasets (two conditional, three unconditional) conducted against seven baselines show that FracTS consistently outperforms baselines across marginal distribution and predictive ML utility, and correlation preservation in the unconditional setting. In particular, the predictive ML utility is averagely improved by 100.0% and 75.0-81.0% for unconditional and conditional time series datasets compared to the best baseline (relative error computed on dataset-averaged raw values). | **[KPI-Aware Multimodal Anomaly Detection for 5G and Open RAN Telemetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25510v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable anomaly detection is a deployment bottleneck for 5G/Open RAN observability and emerging self-optimizing 6G systems, where hundreds of heterogeneous KPIs, severe class imbalance, and strict alert budgets make purely numerical scores brittle. | **[Paloma: Phase-Conditioned Residual Modulation for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25392v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Explicitly modeling periodicity has proven to be an efficient approach for time series forecasting, where the residual, obtained after removing the periodic component from the time series, is typically treated as unstructured noise. However, in this work, we show that such residuals still exhibit structured, \textit{phase-dependent} deviations, as supported by both empirical observations and theoretical analysis. This suggests that residuals should not be treated merely as unstructured noise, but instead as \textit{phase-conditioned signals} whose variations arise in both direction and magnitude. Motivated by this perspective and inspired by phase and amplitude modulation in classical signal processing, we propose \textit{Paloma}, a simple yet effective framework for modeling residual deviations. Specifically, Paloma comprises two complementary modules: \textit{a Phase Rotation module}, which performs phase-conditioned rotations to capture directional changes in the feature space, and \textit{an Amplitude Scaling module}, which applies phase-conditioned affine transformations to model magnitude variations. We further abstract the two modules into a general-purpose plugin, termed the \textit{Paloma technique}, which can be readily integrated into existing forecasting architectures to modulate input sequences in a phase-aware manner, enabling backbone models to capture phase-dependent variations in both direction and magnitude. Extensive experiments on twelve datasets demonstrate that Paloma achieves state-of-the-art and robust forecasting performance. Additional results show that the Paloma technique consistently improves a wide range of baseline models, validating its effectiveness as a general-purpose component. Our Code is available at \url{https://github.com/TSTS13/Paloma}.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026 spotlight</p></details> |
| **[Patch-Level Tokenization with CNN Encoders and Attention for Improved Transformer Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.12467v4)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Transformer-based models have shown strong performance in time-series forecasting by leveraging self-attention to model long-range temporal dependencies. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed framework consistently outperforms a convolutional baseline under increased temporal context and remains competitive with a strong patch-based Transformer model. These findings indicate that structured patch-level tokenization provides a scalable and effective representation for multivariate time-series forecasting, particularly when longer input sequences are considered.</p></details> | <details><summary>7 pag...</summary><p>7 pages, 1 figure(s), 6 table(s)</p></details> |
| **[Data-driven forecasting of tropical cloud forest phenology: A dynamical systems and machine learning hybrid approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25283v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Forecasting how species and ecosystems respond to climate change is a critical challenge in ecology, particularly in hyper-diverse tropical biomes. Our results clearly suggest that the synergy between dynamical systems and machine learning effectively captures the non-linear complexities of ecological time series. | **[EasyResearch: Honest Regression, Forecasting and Signal Classification for Researchers Who Do Not Program](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25263v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>EasyClassifier showed that researchers who do not program can obtain honest classification results when the correct evaluation is the default. Most applied studies, however, predict a number, forecast a series or classify recordings, and each task has its own way of producing scores that do not hold on new data. We present EasyResearch, open-source analysis cores for regression, time-series forecasting and signal classification, and EasyResearch Desktop, a Windows application that runs these three tasks and EasyClassifier's classification without code. All four follow the same rules: everything learned from data is learned on training data only, models keep their published defaults with fixed seeds, the selected model receives a separate final score, forecasts are never evaluated on shuffled data, and every run writes a report with a Methods paragraph and the references for every method used. We measured whether the reported scores are honest by keeping part of each dataset untouched. In regression (9 public datasets, 90 runs), the common practice overstated R^2 by +2.2 points and the best comparison score by +2.5, while EasyResearch's final score was -1.0; screening predictors on all rows before cross-validation, which EasyResearch does not offer, reported R^2 = 0.27 on pure noise against -0.44 on new data. In forecasting (15 series), shuffled cross-validation over windows of past values promised errors 19% smaller than its chosen models achieved in the untouched future, while EasyResearch's final-period score was unbiased to within 1.5%; the models EasyResearch chose had 14% lower future error, and on random walks shuffled cross-validation chose a machine-learning model in 5 of 5 series, each worse in the future than repeating the last value, which EasyResearch chose 4 times. In signal classification (4 UCR datasets) one method won every comparison and neither estimate was biased.</p></details> |  |
| **[Vector Copula-Based Markov Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25129v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We propose the Vector Copula-based Markov Model (VCBMM) for describing multivariate time series with nonlinear dependence. We further assume the Clayton copula for serial dependence and the multivariate Gaussian distribution for the marginals. The performance of the proposed model is illustrated through simulation studies. In real data analysis, we apply the model to bivariate daily stock returns from the 2008 global financial crisis (2008 GFC) and the 2020 COVID-19 pandemic (2020 COVID-19).</p></details> |  |

To address these issues, this study proposes LC-TSRNet for seamless reconstruction of annual MODIS SR products. In the first stage, an annual land cover map is introduced as prior knowledge, and CSPM is designed by leveraging the spectral similarity within identical land cover types, thereby providing prior spectral constraints for missing regions. In the second stage, to address large-area, persistent, and overlapping gaps in MODIS imagery, SMRM is designed to adaptively exploit and integrate multi-scale spatiotemporal contextual information. Meanwhile, to address the limited collaborative utilization of spatio-temporal-spectral information, 3DAM is designed to impose cross-dimensional joint constraints. Considering the lack of publicly available MODIS sample datasets, this study constructs the AIRMOD-TSR dataset. Extensive experimental results show that LC-TSRNet outperforms the comparison methods and achieves better reconstruction accuracy and spectral fidelity. Innovations follow a conditional exponential-family prior over observed auxiliary context and regime embeddings. We train by maximizing the ELBO and estimate uncertainty in both latent trajectories and forecasts. Under explicit assumptions, we prove partial identifiability up to the stated class and provide the full proof in the appendix. We evaluate factor recovery on synthetic DGPs, intervention behavior on synthetic SCMs, and forecasting on real benchmarks with CRPS and MSE.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in Proceedings of The 1st Symposium on Probabilistic Machine Learning (ProbML 2026), PMLR 327:189-213. 25 pages. | **[HCFM: Helmholtz Component Flow Matching for Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25059v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Flow Matching (FM) is an efficient simulation-free approach for training flow-based generative models, making it a promising foundation for scalable time-series anomaly detection (TSAD). However, standard FM learns a monolithic vector field in which nominal transport and local density-changing behavior are entangled. This creates a central challenge for TSAD; local expansion and contraction carry crucial anomaly-sensitive information, yet isolating this signal requires expensive Jacobian-trace estimation over the entire field. We propose \emph{Helmholtz Component Flow Matching} (HCFM), an unsupervised TSAD framework that parameterizes the vector field prediction network with separate transport, compression, and residual heads. The transport head is constrained to be divergence-free, so divergence estimation is restricted to the compression head, while a lightweight residual head absorbs the remaining mismatch. This structured parameterization improves scalability and detection performance by separating volume-preserving nominal transport from density-changing compression, yielding cleaner anomaly signals from distinct deviations of nominal behavior. Experiments on large-scale univariate and multivariate TSAD benchmarks show that HCFM consistently improves detection accuracy while maintaining efficient inference compared with 16 baselines. Clustering the embeddings recovers movement structure tied to progress toward the target without any labels, which also transfers to unseen episodes. On gait data, the embeddings outperform PCA and autoencoder baselines under a nonlinear probe. However, removing the recurrent connection does not yield a measurable loss on the reaching task, and simple per-channel summary statistics classify walking speed at least as well as the embeddings. Under review at Transactions on Machine Learning Research (TMLR)</p></details> |
| **[FreshCast: Timely and Complementary Retrieval for Frozen Time Series Forecasters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07834v3)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented forecasting for Web and online services supplements a forecaster with the continuations of historical segments that resemble the current context. We propose FreshCast, a plug-in retrieval framework for frozen forecasters: a non-parametric streaming memory keeps writing in new observations, relational kernel regression weights candidate readouts into a memory forecast, and its correction weight is calibrated in closed form on the validation segment. Under a simplified generative model, we characterize the optimal combination gain through the second-order relation between forecaster error and memory correction, and show that periodic memory information can become redundant once the forecaster captures it. Across seven datasets, ten forecasters, and three input lengths, FreshCast reduces average MSE for every forecaster and input length, by 14.0% and 5.7% at L = 96 and 720, and achieves lower average MSE than the evaluated retrieval-augmented and online updating baselines in their comparison settings. For a frozen forecaster, similarity alone is not enough: useful retrieval must stay timely and provide information that the forecaster still misses.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 4 figures, 6 tables, Work in progress</p></details> |
